#104. Beijing promised to 'fight back' over Taiwan leader's US visit. But this time it has more to lose

An anticipated meeting between Taiwan’s President Tsai Ing-wen and US House Speaker Kevin McCarthy in California this week has sparked concerns of a repeat of the pressure campaign China launched last year when then-Speaker Nancy Pelosi visited Taipei.

At that time, Beijing encircled the island democracy with unprecedented military drills – firing multiple missiles into its surrounding waters and sending dozens of warplanes speeding across a sensitive median line dividing the Taiwan Strait.

It also cut off contact with the United States over a number of issues from military matters to combating climate change, in retaliation for what it viewed as a violation of its sovereignty.

This time, Beijing has already threatened to “resolutely fight back” if a Tsai-McCarthy meeting goes ahead.

It also slammed Washington for allowing Tsai to stopover in the US while en route to and from official visits in Central America, warning it could lead to “serious” confrontation between the two powers.

A defiant Tsai staked out her own ground, pledging as she took off on her 10-day trip not to let “external pressure” stop Taiwan from connecting with the world and like-minded democracies.

But the optics of the meeting, taking place in California and not Taiwan, and its timing – at a particularly thorny moment in China’s foreign relations and ahead of a presidential election in Taiwan that could reset the tone of its relationship with Beijing – may see Beijing tread more carefully this time, or at least not escalate further, analysts say.

“This puts the burden on China not to overreact, because any overreaction is only going to push China further away from the world,” said Yun Sun, director of the China Program at the Stimson Center think tank in Washington.

That doesn’t mean, however, that Beijing won’t be closely watching Tsai’s movements as it calibrates its response – and decides how much military might to flex over her meeting with an American lawmaker on American soil.

The opacity of China’s system – and the potential for competing interests within its vast bureaucracy – also make it difficult to accurately predict its response.

“Every time Taiwan does anything that China doesn’t like, the Chinese react with their own military coercion,” Sun said. But in the current situation, “they have to consider the consequences of overreaction,” she added.

The expected meeting, which McCarthy’s office announced earlier this week would take place on Wednesday, also comes at a precarious moment in US-China relations.

Washington and Beijing are struggling to stabilize their communication amid flaring tensions over issues from a downed suspected Chinese surveillance balloon to semiconductor supply chains – raising the stakes of potential damage to that relationship if Beijing lashes out as it did when Tsai met Pelosi.

Taiwan is still feeling the fallout of that response last August, with Chinese military forces now regularly making incursions over what had previously been an informal but largely respected border of control between Beijing and Taipei in the Taiwan Strait. Taiwan’s official Central News Agency also reported Monday that Tsai would meet with McCarthy, citing Tsai’s presidential office.

But a meeting between Tsai and the leader of the Republican majority in the US House of Representatives, who ranks second in line to the Presidency, would mark another symbolic moment for Taiwan and the US, which only maintain unofficial ties.

For Tsai, who is entering the final year of her two-term presidency, “it’s clearly a capstone event,” according to Wen-Ti Sung, a political scientist at the Australian National University’s Taiwan Studies Program. “She has this image as the Taiwanese president who has taken US-Taiwan relations to new heights, and who … has been able to give Taiwan almost unprecedented international visibility,” he said.

That increased visibility – and enhanced cooperation with the US – has followed China’s mounting pressure on the island, which sits fewer than 110 miles (177 kilometers) from the mainland coast.

China’s Communist Party claims the self-governing island democracy as its own despite never having controlled it, and has vowed to take the island, by force if necessary.

China thinks it's diplomatically isolating Taiwan. It isn't

The party has undertaken a sweeping expansion of its military capabilities over the past decade under leader Xi Jinping – and ramped up its pervasive economic, diplomatic and military pressure on Taiwan.

That’s driven concerns, among some in Washington, that Beijing is preparing for an invasion, though China’s official language still suggests that scenario is not its preferred option for achieving the claimed goal of “reunification.”

It is those pressures – and how to support Taiwan against unilateral actions by Beijing – that are likely to be on the table when Tsai, McCarthy and a bipartisan group of US lawmakers sit down on Wednesday.

Congress has been a pillar of increasing American support for Taiwan in recent years. Lawmakers regularly visit the island and drive bipartisan legislation enhancing support and cooperation.

While the US switched its diplomatic relations to Beijing decades ago, it maintains unofficial ties with Taiwan and is bound by law to provide the democratic island with the means to defend itself.

Under Washington’s longstanding “One China” policy, the US acknowledges China’s position that Taiwan is part of China, but has never officially recognized Beijing’s claim to the island of 23 million.

Though McCarthy does not have Pelosi’s decades-long record of advocacy regarding China, the California Republican is now a leading voice pushing for closer scrutiny of Beijing, and meeting Tsai could help him to burnish that image.

Last month, McCarthy told reporters that meeting Tsai in the US would not impact whether he travels to Taiwan in the future – something he had earlier said he wanted to do.

A meeting in California, on US soil, is widely seen as less likely to provoke Beijing than a McCarthy visit to Taiwan.

Pelosi’s trip – the first from a lawmaker of that rank to the island in 25 years – generated a fever pitch of nationalist and anti-US rhetoric in mainland China.

This time, so far, domestic conversation in China’s heavily controlled media sphere has been significantly muted.

But the stakes remain high – including for Beijing itself – over how it responds, analysts say.

As Taiwan prepares for a presidential election in January, a fierce response could push voters away from Taiwan’s main opposition party Kuomintang (KMT), widely seen as more friendly toward Beijing.

It could also jar with another high profile trip happening now: a tour of mainland China from former Taiwan president and senior KMT member Ma Ying-jeou, the first visit from a current or former Taiwan leader since the end of the Chinese Civil War in 1949.

Ma’s tour is a “once in a half a century opportunity to send a conciliatory message between the two sides, Beijing shouldn’t want to tank that,” said Sung, the political scientist.

What you need to know about China-Taiwan tensions

China is also acutely aware that its actions toward Taiwan are under a significantly brighter global spotlight following the invasion of Ukraine by Russian President Vladimir Putin, a close diplomatic partner of Xi. Putin’s rhetoric over Ukraine has echoes of how Xi speaks of Taiwan.

Beijing has recently sought to position itself as an agent of peace in that conflict – especially as it aims to repair frayed ties with Europe.

This week, as Tsai is expected to meet with McCarthy, French President Emmanuel Macron and European Commission President Ursula von der Leyen will head to China – an important opportunity that Xi may not want to overshadow with military posturing.

An aggressive response also risks stoking confrontation with the US, not yet six months after Xi and US President Joe Biden called to enhance communication during a face-to-face meeting in Bali.

“(A less overtly aggressive response) would imply that Beijing does not wish to escalate tension with the US to a level that can risk getting out of hand,” said Steve Tsang, director of the SOAS China Institute in London.

“A reset in US-China ties is not on the agenda, but an easing of tension is not beyond the realm of possibility.”

#102. Iran says it targeted US military radar in Bahrain in retaliatory strike

First direct attack on US military installations for months was in response to US airstrike on communications tower

Iran’s Islamic Revolutionary Guard Corps says it has targeted and destroyed a US early-warning radar in Bahrain in response to a US airstrike on a communications tower in Sirik overlooking the strait of Hormuz. It is Iran’s first direct attack on US military installations for months.

“This is a serious warning to the aggressor enemy to end its aerial mischief and oil smuggling in the strait of Hormuz,” the IRGC said. “In the event of repeated violations and aggression, the IRGC aerospace force is on full alert to confront the enemy without constraint.”

US Central Command denied that the radar system had been destroyed and said US forces had intercepted two Iranian drones headed for Bahrain.

Donald Trump has said in a series of media interviews over the past 24 hours that he will attack Iran only if necessary, claiming that Gulf countries, such as Saudi Arabia, have urged him to hold back from planned large-scale military strikes.

Trump said he had asked the vice-president, JD Vance, to end his interest in negotiating a new deal with Iran and instead focus on corruption and fraud in Democrat-run cities and states.

Vance had been seen by the Iranians as the most trustworthy figure close to Trump, but they had rejected his request for a personal meeting with their negotiators to discuss the terms of a peace deal.

Vance has been at the forefront of Republicans saying the war was the cause of the widespread Republican losses in the midterms. Trump said he had been disappointed in the American public because higher diesel prices were a small price to pay for preventing Iran from acquiring a nuclear weapon.

An opinion poll in the UK showed 65% of the British public would oppose the US resuming the war in Iran, and only 14% would back it. The poll was conducted by YouGov for the Council for Arab-British Understanding (Caabu).

It also found 54% oppose UK bases being used by the US to mount attacks on Iran, with 29% in favour. Support for additional UK sanctions against Iran was 45%, with 22% opposed.

The poll was conducted on Tuesday and Wednesday this week, before two Iranians were found guilty on Thursday of spying and planning attacks on journalists in the UK. The Iranian embassy has rejected the court judgment as an attempt to advance a political agenda.

Chris Doyle, the director of Caabu, said: “The British public are sceptical about any war on Iran and what it might achieve. What might make the Burnham government take note is the very high level of opposition to any further war amongst 2024 Labour voters, at 79%.”

Iran’s Assembly of Experts, a body of elected Islamic jurists responsible for choosing the supreme leader, has become the first governing institution in Iran to call for a review of the country’s defence doctrine, potentially leading to religious permission for Iran to try to acquire a nuclear weapon.

Get the most important US headlines and highlights emailed direct to you every morning

In a statement released on Thursday, the assembly called for a review of Iran’s membership of the nuclear non-proliferation treaty, describing the move as an “unavoidable necessity” and a “public and national demand”.

The intervention carries a religious as much as political significance because the 88-strong assembly comprises senior clerics, most of whom are supposed to be qualified to exercise independent Islamic legal reasoning.

Iran has had a longstanding prohibition on nuclear weapons, based on a fatwa issued by the late supreme leader Ali Khamenei, but the relevance of the fatwa in the wake of the US attacks on Iran has been questioned widely in political and security circles.

Whether Iran has the stockpiles of uranium or technology to build a nuclear weapon is contested.

The UN nuclear inspectorate has expressed alarm about a continued lack of information from Iran about its nuclear sites. It said: “Our concerns are growing over Iran’s refusal to allow access to design information or inspections of the Isfahan facility. We do not know the exact location of the Isfahan uranium enrichment facility or whether it contains nuclear materials. It is imperative that we carry out our verification activities in Iran without further delay.”

#101. Whatever happened to the $300,000 diamond that went missing in F1 crash?

Where were you between the hours of 1pm and 3pm on 23 May 2004?

If you were in Monaco, consider yourself a suspect - or at least a witness - in Formula 1's biggest unsolved mystery.

A $300,000 diamond was last seen stuck to the front of an F1 car before it crashed on the first lap of the Grand Prix and it still has not been found 22 years later.

Did someone pick it up? Was it swept up with all the debris? Did it slip down a drain? Was it even a real diamond?

The reason why a 1.4 carat diamond was attached to the most delicate part of a car that reaches 200mph in a race was straightforward: publicity.

Jaguar Racing's results on the track had been mediocre at best since becoming an F1 team in 2000 and by 2004 its parent company Ford had decided it would be pulling them out of the sport.

"My job flipped from primarily running the team to primarily selling the team and finding a new owner or they would shut it down," former team principal Tony Purnell tells BBC Sport.

"It was probably the worst year of my life. Particularly because for the first few months I was sworn to secrecy."

His team needed some good PR to divert attention away from performance and improve its appeal.

They had enjoyed a successful promotional weekend in Silverstone the previous season thanks to a tie-up with Terminator 3 and so director of communications Nav Sidhu was looking to recreate the magic with another bit of help from a movie.

"I'd heard on the grapevine, around about the winter of 2003, that they were making Ocean's Twelve and we discovered that they were shooting some of the scenes in Monte Carlo," Sidhu says.

"One thing led to another and before you knew it, we had agreed [a deal] with George Clooney, Brad Pitt and Matt Damon."

Sidhu had arranged with legendary Hollywood producer Jerry Weintraub to have the film's branding and stars take over the team for the Monaco weekend.

The film plot centred on an audacious theft of a diamond-encrusted Faberge egg. Sidhu decided he wanted some sparkle on his cars too at the most glamorous race in the F1 calendar.

Sidhu negotiated with diamond company Steinmetz that the two Jaguar cars would have gems - which would today be worth about $1m in total - attached to them.

What happened next, though, was not part of the script.

There was huge media interest in the appearance of the Hollywood stars

While the champagne corks were popping on nearby mega yachts in the principality, the diamonds were being hand-delivered into the garages. There were two that would be mounted on to the car and a larger gem just for the photos.

The diamonds for the cars were encased by steel and fixed to the nose cones. One on experienced driver Mark Webber's car and one on rookie Christian Klien's.

Webber was starting from 11th place in the grid and Klien - in only his sixth race - was starting from 14th. They weren't expected to challenge for the podium, but they were expected to bring the diamonds back.

Jaguar were given two different types of diamond for the weekend - one for promotional purposes (on the left) and one to be welded into the cars (on the right)

Monaco's street circuit is demanding, with narrow roads, tight turns and a tunnel.

Overtaking is difficult and crashes are common - as Klien soon found out.

In a chaotic first lap, the Austrian made contact with one of the Jordan cars exiting the Mirabeau corner. This damaged the front of his car - where the diamond was - and led to a slow collision with the barriers at the famous Loews hairpin.

Klien climbed out unhurt but the front of the car was crumpled.

It was not long before Sidhu had the diamond company on the phone asking if they would get their diamond back.

"I can't remember what I answered, but it certainly wasn't 'yes'," he says.

He couldn't even get on to the track to have a look as the race had another 76 laps to go and only then would the car - which had been lifted off the track by a crane and left behind the barriers - return to the garage.

"I was consumed with getting to that spot as quickly as I possibly could," Sidhu says.

"That hour and a half waiting for the race toâ¯finish felt like a day and a half, every single lap was just like pulling teeth."

By the time he had the chance to have a look, there was no sign of the diamond.

Sidhu completely denies the rumours of a fake diamond.

"I've learned that any diamond company is not going to go out there, particularly in the public domain, and use fake diamonds because clearly there's a high risk of being caught," he says.

"Had we used a fake diamond in that car and what took place took place, then clearly the embarrassment and the disrepute it would bring Jaguar, and Formula 1 for that matter, was too high risk.

"There's been loads of conspiracy theories about was it a real diamond or was Christian told to crash his car and I think that's what's given the story its life - there's a mystery to this story and that mystery will always breed different theories."

Purnell backs up the legitimacy of the diamond, recalling a conversation he had with the managing director of the team, David Pitchforth.

"David said, 'Tony, it was definitely real because when they came to the office with it, I picked the diamond up, went to the window and scratched the window' because that's how to tell.

"Hearing that from Dave, I'm in no doubt it was the real thing that was put on the car.

"I don't know if one of our mechanics was switched on enough to make a fake,â¯but I don't think so."

"I think somebody probably just picked it up, put it in their pocket and... they're probably smiling and thinking, 'it's in my drawer'," says Purnell.

"I do think also that a fairly large diamond zooming through at 100 miles an hour probably shattered. Somebody's got a little bit, somebody else has got another [little bit].

"Maybe, sadly, just some guy was sweeping up the road or one of those trucks with the brushes and it all went down the sewer.

"It did occur to me that the ultimate thing would be if Clooney, Pitt and Damon had contrived to steal it because it was so in line with Ocean's Eleven. But that was a fantasy too far."

There would have been hundreds, if not thousands, of people in the area because within an hour or so of the end of the race the circuit would have been returned to public roads.

Sidhu is pretty certain about what happened to the diamond: "Someone's picked it up.

"What people tend to do is, believe it or not, pick up bits of tyre rubber, you know, claim that as Michael Schumacher's front tyre rubber or whatever they can find.

"Somebody may have picked it up not having any idea it came off a car.

"There's a lot of diamonds in Monte Carlo. There's a lot of people walking around Monte Carlo with a lot of jewellery and a lot of stones.

"Somebody dropped a diamond in Monte Carlo. Well, they're not going to miss that, are they? Probably not."

Sidhu says the cost of the damage to the car was more than the cost of the diamond, which reports at the time said was uninsured.

And points and a good result in the race were more valuable to the team and individuals than a diamond or the publicity a crash would bring. It was simply not worth crashing on purpose.

"For us, bringing two cars home, ideally in the points,â¯had far bigger commercial benefits to us in terms of constructors' points, broadcast money,â¯and more importantly, trying to retain our sponsors and trying to protect the integrity of theâ¯team," Sidhu explains.

Klien drove straight into the barriers right in front of the marshals so would not have had the chance to start looking for a diamond or picking it up even if he had wanted to.

"Christian didn't try and say all the brakes failed or anything like that, he's just said 'I made a rookie mistake'," says Purnell.

"He was a young guy, first time, made a mistake, it happens. Doesn't usually happen with a $300,000 diamond flying into the crowd or wherever it went, but I'm sure Christian wasn't really aware of the diamond.

"No amount of money would have got Christian to do that [crash on purpose]. He wanted to make a success of what he was doing."

While events were unfolding, Purnell had not really been thinking about the diamond.

His main concern that day was that one of his drivers had crashed and the other also did not finish, and that this was not a good look in terms of pleasing current backers or attracting potential buyers.

"When Christian went into the wall and then later Mark retired, my entire thinking was 'Oh God. What am I going to say?' The chairman of HSBC [Jaguar's then sponsors] was there, Sir John Bond. Jackie [Stewart] was there.

"It was all about 'how am I going to explain another bad performance? How am I going to say 'just stick with the plan? We know what we're doing, it will take time' because sponsors don't have time. They want success."

In the end, the missing diamond came to the rescue.

"No-one mentioned Jarno Trulli winning the Grand Prix, no-one asked how the Jaguars did. No-one was interested that Mark Webber's car broke as well. It was, 'where's the diamond?'" says Purnell.

"I remember talking to somebody in Detroit saying, 'fantastic publicity over here, you know we never hear anything about Formula 1 but it's on the newscasts, it's in the papers - fantastic'."

Asked if the diamond mystery had helped the eventual sale of the team to Red Bull six months later, he replies: "I think that helped quite a lot because it buzzed the Jaguar team and showed that huge publicity was possible even if you weren't doing so well."

Red Bull went on to become hugely successful, winning eight drivers' championships and six constructors' championships since joining F1.

"You couldn't design a better PR weekend," says Purnell.

Even if it was not quite how it was planned and there is still the small matter of a missing diamond.

#100. Eisenkot accuses Netanyahu of ‘racist’ campaign against Israel’s Arab minority

Statement is rare instance of a prominent Jewish political leader taking a strong stand in defence of Arab Israelis

Israeli prime ministerial hopeful Gadi Eisenkot accused caretaker premier Benjamin Netanyahu on Friday of fuelling a “racist” campaign against the country’s Arab minority, after he sought to delegitimise the community’s lawmakers by linking them to Hamas.

Eisenkot’s statement was a rare instance of a prominent Jewish political leader taking a strong stand in defence of Arab Israelis, who make up more than 20% of the population.

Two Arab-majority parties, Ra’am and the Joint List, secured a combined 19 seats in last month’s election, winning nearly a sixth of the 120-seat parliament and potentially emerging as kingmakers in efforts by Eisenkot to form a government.

“Arab citizens of Israel are an integral part of the Israeli story,” Eisenkot wrote on X on Friday.

“They have been subjected to a racist and aggressive campaign of delegitimisation, fuelled by the outgoing prime minister and those around him for political purposes,” he added.

“The attempt to marginalise them and vilify them collectively is a profound violation of the principles of civic responsibility.”

Netanyahu and his allies have repeatedly criticised leaders of Arab parties for refusing to describe Hamas as a “terror group” after its unprecedented 7 October 2023 attack on Israel.

All leaders of Arab parties have condemned the attack, in which Arab Israelis – descendants of Palestinians who remained in what became Israel in 1948 – were also among those killed.

Netanyahu has also repeatedly alleged electoral fraud by Arab-majority parties, but electoral authorities have rejected the claims.

In a speech, Netanyahu lamented that the two Arab lists refused to recognise Israel as a Jewish state, although Ra’am’s leader, Mansour Abbas, has done so in the past.

He further claimed that they sought to establish either an “Islamic state” or a “nationalist Palestinian state”.

During the same ceremony, the national security minister, Itamar Ben Gvir, heckled Joint List’s leader, Yousef Jabareen, during his oath of allegiance, accusing him of having “pledged allegiance to Hamas”.

“It is entirely legitimate to disagree with representatives of the Arab parties, even to strongly disapprove of some of them. But it is neither legitimate nor acceptable to malign an entire community, and I will not allow it,” Eisenkot said.

President Isaac Herzog tasked Eisenkot with forming a new government on Sunday after consultations in which a majority of political parties, including the two Arab lists, recommended him for the role.

But one of Eisenkot’s rightwing allies, Avigdor Lieberman, has said he will back out of the coalition if Arab Israelis are part of it.

If Eisenkot fails to form a government within 28 days, he may request a 14-day extension. Should he still fall short, the mandate would pass to another lawmaker, most likely Netanyahu.

#98. Tourists Brave a Muddy Jungle Trek to See a Famed World War II Wreck

Seven Chinese tourists, a dozen villagers and a couple of journalists forded streams and bushwhacked to the downed plane of Isoroku Yamamoto, the Japanese admiral. Machetes proved handy.

Seven Chinese tourists, a dozen villagers and a couple of journalists forded streams and bushwhacked to the downed plane of Isoroku Yamamoto, the Japanese admiral. Machetes proved handy.

Wading through streams and bushes to the plane wreckage, where a tourist posed for pictures.Credit...

Reporting from Bougainville, Papua New Guinea

Halfway through the morning, I’m being tossed around like a popcorn kernel in the back of a troop carrier playing an eclectic medley of Backstreet Boys and Papua New Guinean jams.

We’re traveling along what feels more like a collection of loose rocks, arranged to be roughly vehicle width, than a road. I’ve lost count of the number of river crossings, including one where the water lapped a few inches from the car’s windows. And this is during a dry spell.

Our caravan is headed for an unlikely tourist destination deep in the jungle on Bougainville Island, an autonomous region of Papua New Guinea in the Solomon Sea.

The piecemeal remnants of a Mitsubishi G4M1 — Betty, as the planes were code-named by the Allies in World War II — have remained for some 80 years at the crash site of Japanese Navy Adm. Isoroku Yamamoto. This is the spot where Admiral Yamamoto, mastermind of the Pearl Harbor attack, archvillain to Americans in the war, a respected and memorialized figure to the Japanese, met his end in 1943.

Bougainville was occupied by the Japanese in early 1942, in the months after Pearl Harbor, as the Imperial Navy pushed into the South Pacific, with Admiral Yamamoto at the helm. In April 1943, Allied forces intercepted and deciphered Japanese communication on the admiral’s travels, and American P-38 Lightning fighters gunned down his plane over southern Bougainville, a feat the Americans triumphantly dubbed Operation Vengeance.

#97. Neither war nor peace in Tigray - is Ethiopia's latest conflict really over?

Pro-government forces marched into Tigray's capital less than two weeks after the war began

Running a hair salon in Mekelle, capital of Ethiopia's troubled northern Tigray region, is not easy at the moment.

"People can't prioritise beauty over daily basic needs. For many residents, thinking about self-care is a luxury, at a time when they can't feed their kids," Firut Me'ar, who runs a salon in the city, tells the BBC.

After a brief war, the city is under the control of the national army but life is far from normal, says Firut, a single mother of a 10-year-old daughter.

"Things are better than during the war, but people still have no money, no morale. Business is almost non-existent," she says, her voice shaking with emotion.

Above all, she says people are worried about what the future holds. "There is uncertainty about what comes next."

Fighting broke out in late September between the Tigray People's Liberation Front (TPLF), the party that had run Tigray for decades, and the national army - a resumption of the two-year civil war that officially ended in 2022.

But within less than two weeks, federal forces marched triumphantly into Mekelle. The TPLF made what it called a "strategic withdrawal" from the city and Tigray's other main towns so the army encountered little resistance. Hundreds of people are reported to have died in the conflict, including dozens killed by drone strikes and bombing raids by the air force.

Ethiopian government officials and supporters argue that the war is effectively over and that the only remaining task is to clear Tigray of TPLF elements.

Getachew Reda, a senior TPLF official who switched sides and is now an adviser to the prime minister, declared that the war in Tigray had ended and that stabilising the region should now be the immediate priority.

Cameroon Hudson, an analyst and consultant on African peace and security, says there are various reasons for the TPLF's rapid defeat.

"The TPLF has lost popular support at home. It is fragmented, it does not possess the advanced military technology necessary to match the ENDF [Ethiopian National Defence Forces]," he says. "It has lost many of its generals, strategic thinkers, finest diplomats and fighters, either through defections or arrests. The infighting within the TPLF leadership has caused significant damage."

Abraha Hailezgi, a scholar at Mekelle University, agrees. "The army lacks a clear political vision. In recent years, it has become associated with coercion, abuses, forced recruitment and arbitrary actions. As a result, many people have come to view the TPLF army as an adversary rather than a force acting on their behalf," he says.

Speaking by telephone from an undisclosed location, TPLF spokesperson Mikael Asgedom denies that the group has lost.

Instead, he describes it as a deliberate tactical and strategic retreat. "This is not a defeat," he tells the BBC.

Dozens of people were killed in drone strikes during the short conflict

He says the TPLF "intentionally allowed the ENDF to advance deeper into the heartland of Tigray", where it can be isolated and confronted on favourable terrain. "We chose not to fight on terms favourable to the enemy."

The TPLF is preparing for a major counteroffensive, he says, adding that TPLF fighters have launched attacks against Ethiopian forces in several locations following their withdrawal from the region's towns, arguing that these operations demonstrated the group's continued military capability.

It is hard to get verified information from Tigray and the BBC is unable to report from there. But phone lines and the internet, which were cut when fighting broke out, have now been restored.

Residents in different parts of Tigray tell the BBC that fighting is continuing in central, northern and north-western parts of Tigray, with the use of heavy artillery and drone strikes.

Eyewitnesses around Sheraro, a town near the Eritrean border, report heavy fighting involving artillery and ground forces in recent days.

"We could hear heavy artillery fire from the Sheraro direction. People are concerned because there is no sense of security," a businessman tells the BBC on condition of anonymity. He adds that military convoys from both sides had moved towards Sheraro, while a motorcycle operator in the southern town of Yechila says military checkpoints are still active.

Memories of the devastating 2020-2022 Tigray war remain raw for many residents.

The conflict saw widespread human rights abuses and allegations of ethnic cleansing in Western Tigray, while estimates suggest the war claimed hundreds of thousands of lives.

As soldiers from Eritrea, accused of some of the worst atrocities in the previous conflict, and Ethiopia have reappeared in Tigray's towns, many residents describe a profound sense of fear and anxiety, saying the sight of the troops has reopened still-fresh wounds.

Military sources aligned with the federal army say operations against the TPLF are continuing, with recent fighting and troop movements reported in northern Tigray, near the Eritrean border, as well as across central Tigray's rugged highlands. They claim the TPLF's capabilities are being steadily degraded.

Security analysts and observers agree that unrest is likely to continue for some time. "I see a situation in which there could be a prolonged war in Tigray," Yared Tibebu, a well-known Ethiopian commentator based in the US, tells the BBC.

Yared argues that the TPLF has a long history of waging protracted armed struggles and believes the movement is once again preparing for a lengthy conflict. He believes that once the group has reorganised its military and political structures, it may attempt to resume offensive operations.

However he believes this will not happen for some time because of internal divisions - a suggestion dismissed by TPLF spokesperson Mikael.

The army says it seized all these weapons from the TPLF during the fighting

Hudson rejects the idea that Tigray is heading towards a prolonged war but agrees that the region may not be truly peaceful for some time. "The TPLF may continue a low-level insurgency and guerrilla campaign, but that is not war. I do not think there will be a wider-scale conflict between the federal forces and the TPLF."

He says the TPLF no longer possesses the size, strength or military capability required to effectively confront federal forces, which he says have significantly expanded and modernised in recent years. "The TPLF has also lost much of its public support and cannot easily rebuild its ranks through recruitment," he says.

Yared, on the other hand, points to the TPLF's history of overcoming setbacks as evidence that the movement could recover to sustain a prolonged struggle. "The TPLF has overcome many major challenges in the past. It is wrong to conclude that it has no public support. It is a political movement with a history stretching back more than 50 years," he says.

Most Mekelle residents only have money for the basic necessities at the moment

What Yared sees as a strength, Hudson sees as a weakness, saying the TPLF is still "trapped in the past", and continues to behave as a dominant party within Tigray, even though the situation has changed. "It is a movement that has not evolved, modernised or expanded."

While Hudson argues that the TPLF is not equipped to sustain a major military campaign, the TPLF spokesman says the group obtains weapons and logistical supplies from the Ethiopian military. "We seize weapons from our enemy, and the weaponry that has entered Tigray will, sooner or later, become ours."

In early October, eyewitnesses told the BBC they had seen Eritrean troops in Tigray amid reports they were backing the TPLF. However, they appear to have returned home and Hudson thinks the country would be wary of becoming further embroiled in the conflict.

"First, Eritrea does not have unlimited resources. Second, the TPLF is no more an effective military force, and Eritrean leaders may have concluded that supporting it at this stage would not yield significant military gains," he says. "Third, Eritrea would have to consider the risk of provoking a confrontation with Ethiopia if it openly backs the TPLF."

Sources in the Ethiopia army tell the BBC that senior members of the TPLF's political leadership were briefly based in Eritrea, however, they say the authorities ordered them to leave in early November, shortly after President Isaias Afwerki returned from Russia. According to the sources, the move was driven by concerns that Ethiopia could use their presence as a reason to invade.

Hudson also argues that Eritrea's renewed diplomatic engagement with the US, whose relations with the TPLF have long been strained, could further discourage Asmara from providing meaningful support to the movement.

In September, seven Ethiopian rebel groups led by the TPLF announced the formation of a new coalition called the Alliance for Survival, aimed at removing Prime Minister Abiy Ahmed's government.

Seven rebel groups from across Ethiopia have announced a new alliance

Yared believes the alliance could give the TPLF the strength to keep on fighting. "Its members can combine their strengths. The TDF [the TPLF's military wing] has leadership experience and organisational capacity, while Fano [an ethnic Amhara militia] is believed to have between 70,000 and 80,000 fighters.

"If conditions emerge that allow the forces to co-ordinate effectively, the TPLF's current difficulties may not last long," he says.

Hudson, however, rejects that argument, insisting that the alliance is unlikely to alter the balance of power. "I don't think the TPLF can be saved by this alliance. The ethnic militias have not demonstrated the capacity, either individually or collectively, to pose a serious threat to federal forces. We should not underestimate the strength of the federal army," he says.

Abraha from Mekelle University argues that the conflict in Tigray will only truly be over if the federal government addresses the region's feeling that it has been marginalised, both economically and politically by the current government.

However, many Tigrayan elites and political observers remain wary of the federal government's intentions. They question whether the authorities in Addis Ababa are genuinely seeking to address Tigray's problems, or whether they are taking advantage of the region's weakness to boost their influence.

In the meantime, many ordinary people in Tigray say their priority is peace.

"Peace seems slow in coming. Most people are tired of politics. Whatever happens, we only want peace and a chance to rebuild our lives," says Firut from her beauty salon in Mekelle.

#96. Suspected Chinese spy balloon was able to transmit information back to Beijing

The Chinese spy balloon that transited the US earlier this year was able to capture imagery and collect some signals intelligence from US military sites, a source familiar with the matter tells CNN.

The balloon was able to transmit information back to Beijing in real time, the source said, and the US government still does not know for sure whether the Chinese government could wipe the balloon’s data as it received it. That raises questions about whether there is intelligence the balloon was able to gather that the US still doesn’t know about.

Still, the intelligence community has not been overly concerned about the information the balloon was able to gather, the person said, because it is not much more sophisticated than what Chinese satellites are able to glean as they orbit over similar locations.

A US intelligence official told CNN on Monday that “although analysis of the wreckage of the High Altitude Balloon remains ongoing, thus far, its flight over the United States does not appear to have provided critical new insights to the People’s Republic of China.”

The US also knew what the balloon’s path would be and was able to protect sensitive sites and censor some signals before the balloon was able to pick them up, officials have said.

As CNN has reported, the US intelligence community last year developed a method of tracking what it says is a fleet of these Chinese balloons operating across the globe, controlled by the Chinese military.

The FBI is still examining the balloon, but so far officials have been able to glean additional information about how the device worked, including the algorithms used for the balloon’s software and how it is powered and designed.

CNN has reached out to the National Security Council at the White House and the Pentagon for comment. NBC was first to report on the new intelligence.

The balloon first crossed into US airspace over Alaska in late January before passing through Canada and down into Montana, where it hovered for a few days, leading the US to believe it was trying to surveil sensitive military sites, like Malmstrom Air Force Base in Montana. It was eventually shot down by the US off the East Coast on February 4, and the incident further escalated tensions between Washington and Beijing, including the postponement of a diplomatic visit by US Secretary of State Antony Blinken to China.

A senior State Department official said in February that, as the balloon floated across the US, it “was capable of conducting signals intelligence collection operations.”

Gen. Glen VanHerck, the commander of US Northern Command and NORAD, said at the time that the US “did not assess” that the balloon “presented a significant collection hazard beyond what already exists in actionable technical means from the Chinese.”

The surveillance program, which includes a number of similar balloons, is in part run out of the small Chinese province of Hainan, officials told CNN. The US does not know the precise size of the fleet of Chinese surveillance balloons, but sources tell CNN that the program has conducted at least two dozen missions over at least five continents in recent years.

Roughly half a dozen of those flights have been within US airspace, although not necessarily over US territory, according to one official familiar with the intelligence.

China has maintained that the balloon was actually just a weather balloon thrown off course, and the US has been assessing the possibility that it was not deliberately maneuvered into the continental US by the Chinese government, CNN has reported.

Still, China did maintain some ability to maneuver the balloon, officials believe. And once the balloon was over Montana, China appeared to take advantage of its position to loiter over sensitive sites and try to collect intelligence.

This story has been updated with additional reporting.

CNN’s Alex Marquardt contributed to this report.

#95. ‘Ahead of his time’: AI helps unlock discoveries hidden in notebooks of man who transformed maths

Kurt Gödel left 1,009 pages of densely written text – much of it in an obsolete shorthand – which is now said to contain hundreds of proved theorems

The Austrian logician Kurt Gödel was one of the most original thinkers of the 20th century. His two incompleteness theorems, published in 1931, transformed mathematics and paved the way for the digital age.

After his seminal results of the 1930s, Gödel published only a few papers. His unpublished work, however, runs to tens of thousands of pages, and is of great interest to historians of science.

Since 2017, the Finnish logician Jan von Plato has led a project at Helsinki University to understand the contents of 16 notebooks that Gödel wrote in the early 1940s. This material – the “workbooks” – is 1,009 pages of densely written text about maths and logic. Much of it is written in a German shorthand, Gabelsberger, that was obsolete even when Gödel was using it.

Progress in the mathematical understanding of the text was painfully slow – about a page a week – until September, when von Plato enlisted the help of the AI model Claude. Within a couple of days, Claude had provided a comprehensive digest of the entire material, revealing Gödel had proved hundreds of theorems that no one knew about.

The most famous logician of modern times had, in fact, discovered significantly more than anyone had thought.

“I was flabbergasted,” said von Plato. “The workbooks not only contain dozens of anticipations of results not known until the 70s and 80s, but they also contain 14 themes in logic that have not been pursued. Gödel was far ahead of his time.”

Gödel changed the course of logic because he revealed the limits of mathematical knowledge. His incompleteness theorems show that in all mathematical systems there are true statements that can neither be proved or disproved. Yet while this result was a blow to previous generations of logicians, who had hoped that all truths were verifiable, Gödel’s work pushed the field in new directions, inspiring Alan Turing to write his 1936 paper that began the theory of modern computation.

In 1940, Gödel fled Austria under the Nazis. He settled in Princeton, New Jersey, and became a close friend of Albert Einstein at the Institute of Advanced Study. Increasingly paranoid in his later years, he refused to eat food that was not cooked by his wife, and died of starvation in 1978.

Von Plato said he had started his Gödel project in 2017 because he was facing mandatory retirement from Helsinki University. “What should I do to make myself indispensable? Oh yes, I’ll start reading Gödel’s enigmatic shorthand pages!” He received a €2.5m (£2.1m) grant from the European Research Council, taught himself Gabelsberger shorthand – “I am one of only six people who can read it in the world” – and assembled a small team.

In August, at a conference in France about AI and theorem proving, von Plato met computer scientists who suggested that he co-opt the help of Claude in trying to make sense of the workbooks. On his return to Helsinki he began the process, showing Claude his transcriptions of the workbooks, as well as six books he had previously written on Gödel and other secondary scientific literature.

“In a couple of days, I got to see a 650-page explanation of the workbooks. It had to be controlled page for page, but it’s basically a work that is impossible to do for human beings,” he said. “Humans read one page at a time and we tend to forget things. It takes us a year to read about 50 pages. We just are not able to keep in mind all the different parts.

“The AI can connect all the pieces in a thousand pages. It can see all of Gödel’s writings as one piece.”

The most pressing stories and debates for Europeans – from identity to economics to the environment

Gödel’s incompleteness theorems revealed the existence of statements whose truths were independent of the system they were written in: that is, they could not be proved or disproved within the system. In 1966, the American mathematician Paul Cohen won the Fields medal, the highest honour in maths, for showing the independence of two well-known mathematical statements: the continuum hypothesis and the axiom of choice.

Gödel reacted to Cohen’s Fields medal by saying that he had already proved the result in the 1940s. Yet when pushed for a proof by his peers, Gödel was unable to reconstruct one from his notes.

The first task von Plato set Claude was to discover if Gödel’s workbooks had a proof of the independence of the axiom of choice, which would reveal that Gödel had been telling the truth in 1966. The answer came back: yes, almost. “He was just one step away,” said von Plato. “There’s one definition that Gödel got wrong, but he had the right definition two years earlier, and so the thought is that he would have arrived at the right definition anyway if he had just continued for a few months. For me, the architecture of his proof is just wonderful. It makes all of these results of set theory follow a uniform pattern.

“What strikes me especially is that he starts from ideas. He’s not like someone who plays with formulas and hopes to arrive somewhere. He has very, very clear ideas, and then he’s methodical. Step after step he goes ahead and he manages to fulfil the aims that he has in this formal work.”

Von Plato said that when Claude presented him with the reconstructed proof of the independence of the axiom of choice, he ran it through the proof assistant Coq, a program that verifies the validity of mathematical proofs, and it passed. Von Plato believes that had Gödel published his work on the axiom of choice, he would have won the 1940 Fields medal. “Maybe he felt that when Cohen got the medal, that that was something he sort of missed.”

In further discussions with Claude, von Plato asked the AI model to extract all theorems in the workbooks for which Gödel either had a proof, had sketched a proof or had an idea for a proof. Claude found 663. When these results were put into Coq: “About 400 were verified as stated, about 200 were repaired, meaning that perhaps the statement of the theorem had to be made more precise, or some assumption had to be added. About 50 were false, and this is no wonder. Gödel himself, when rereading his notes, would occasionally add a marginal remark: falsch! [incorrect] or schlecht! [bad].”

Von Plato added: “I am sure that the continued study of Gödel’s manuscripts will reveal further treasures. The workbooks contain, Claude says, more than a dozen themes in logic and foundations of mathematics that have not been found in the 80-plus years since they were written down. So this is one thing to explore. But there are many others. His physics and cosmology notebooks are an unknown territory I plan to start exploring once I am finished with my present logic projects.”

Joel David Hamkins, a professor of logic at the University of Notre Dame, Indiana, has not seen Claude’s reconstruction of Gödel’s proof of the independence of the axiom of choice, but said that if it stood up “it is remarkable and historically interesting. Although how much is Claude and how much is Gödel is a job for the historians.”

Hamkins said: “The fact that there are things in there that resemble [Cohen’s proof] does not mean he had it. It shows he was on the path. You don’t get credit for work you don’t tell anyone about. But Gödel had so many ground-breaking ideas that it is maybe not surprising that his notebooks are full of exciting things.”

#93. Why midterm vote-counting is taking so long in America's largest state

The results of the Alaska midterm elections might not be known until 18 November

For the last few weeks, in addition to its usual payload of passengers, medicine, and supplies, Alaska Seaplanes has carried some unique cargo: ballots.

The regional airline is a piece of a complex logistical puzzle that sees Alaskans' midterms elections votes cast by mail and in person carried across the vast expanse of America's largest state, which is primarily connected by water and air rather than roads.

This considerable task explains why, a week after most US states knew their results in the midterms - in which the Democrats won back both chambers of Congress from the Republicans - Alaskans are still waiting for the Senate race to be called.

The victor in this close contest between the incumbent Republican Dan Sullivan and his Democratic challenger, former congresswoman Mary Peltola, could remain unknown until 18 November, when officials expect to have counted all eligible ballots.

"We do feel a sense of pride of making sure we do the job right - the really complicated job of getting all these ballots across thousands of miles in Alaska," Andy Kline, the marketing director of Alaska Seaplanes, which serves the south-eastern part of the state, told the BBC.

"We feel like we're part of the last leg of that journey of those ballots."

An Alaska Seaplanes aircraft similar to the ones that transport ballots, along with other cargo and mail, during elections

Some precincts in Alaska are able to communicate their initial results to centralised election offices on election night, allowing state officials and news outlets to report some preliminary vote tallies.

But it takes time for the full picture of how people have voted to emerge.

As well as having the logistical challenge of counting votes from across a state more than six times the size of the UK, there is a delay waiting for mail ballots to arrive, says Charles Stewart, an election expert at the Massachusetts Institute of Technology.

These are allowed to arrive up to 10 days later, or 15 days for overseas voters, if postmarked by election day.

Stewart said that election officials had "chosen to err on the side of getting it right rather than a rush to report the second and third tranches of ballots that get counted".

Regardless of whether ballots were cast in person or by mail, all of them eventually wind up in the state capital of Juneau â which is only reachable by plane or boat â for scanning and counting.

While more urban districts can get their materials on a plane late on election night, it takes longer for smaller or more remote parts of Alaska.

In some places, "you get someone who's basically running the election for their family and their neighbours because it's such a small village", says Scott Kendall, an election lawyer who previously worked on two campaigns for Alaska's other Republican senator, Lisa Murkowski.

In these smaller areas, ballots are sealed up in special envelopes or containers and sent it through the mail - which is where companies like Alaska Seaplanes comes in.

Ballots are put on the planes and sent to Juneau, where they then get dropped at the post office and delivered.

"It's part of our daily routine, just another couple of boxes," Kline said.

Former congresswoman Mary Peltola is the Democratic candidate

Once all the in-person and absentee mail ballots reach Juneau, election officials will complete their review.

If no candidate gets more than 50% of the vote in the first round, officials will quickly use ranked-choice voting to re-allocate votes from the lowest-scoring candidates to their voters' second choices.

Then Alaska will finally declare a winner for its Senate race, as well as contests for governor and US House of Representatives.

Adding to the suspense of who will win the Senate race is the question of whether another Republican candidate called Dan Sullivan, who was allegedly recruited by Democratic operatives, would affect the outcome. Results released so far suggest a low probability.

This week, President Donald Trump alleged Alaska's Senate election had been "rigged" due to the second Sullivan's involvement. His administration is appealing its rejected lawsuit to halt certification of the election over the issue, which a lower court dismissed.

US Senator Dan Sullivan of Alaska is one of two men with the same name who were on the ballot

Meanwhile, a judge has denied an attempt by Peltola's campaign to get information about how many absentee ballots election officials rejected, as well as information about those voters.

While the result of the election is of the greatest importance to many, for people like Kline, the process is also key.

"We're all citizens," he said of himself and his colleagues.

"We all feel like we have skin in the game to make sure that our elections are accurate and timely."

Judge dismisses Trump administration's lawsuit over two Dan Sullivans in Alaska election

Alaska Senate candidate denies knowing about alleged plot to put two Dan Sullivans on ballot

#92. Europe should not walk into Russia’s ‘traps’ on Ukraine talks, EU’s foreign policy chief Kallas says – as it happened

Meanwhile, German tabloid Bild has reported on what it says are the key Russian demands put forward in negotiations with Europe, Ukraine, the US.

It includes a number of maximalist demands which would dramatically alter the security landscape for Ukraine and Europe.

We already knew about some of them – for example, Russia’s demand to keep the occupied Ukrainian territories and the entire Donbas region, demilitarise Ukrainian ports and keep Ukraine out of Nato – but there are also some new ones, including a reported request that Nato should not have any bases in the Baltics and that Finland, which only joined in 2023, should be made to leave the alliance.

The list also includes new proposals on Moldova, which Moscow reportedly wants to turn into a federal republic and then force referendums on the future of two of its regions, Transnistria and Gaugazia.

If this list is true – which we do not know that and have not been able to independently verify it – it is probably fair to say that it’s just not going to work and will only anger Europeans at the time when the US wants to get them formally involved in the process.

Ukraine has been repeatedly clear that it has no intention of giving away the temporarily occupied territories, and even less of signing off the parts that are actually still controlled by Kyiv.

And Finland’s defence minister Antti Häkkänen also made it clear that the demand on Finland’s Nato membership has no chance of succeeding.

“These matters are not up for negotiation. Russia knows that and it’s absolutely outrageous to even suggest this,” he said, quoted by Finnish public broadcaster yle. “Finland will not be forced out [of Nato],” he added.

The chairman of the Finnish parliament’s foreign affairs committee, Johannes Koskinen, told reporters that “if such a paper or demand has been submitted, it will go straight into the bin,” IS reported.

Lithuanian foreign minister Kęstutis Budrys also made it clear earlier this week that Lithuania would block any agreement giving Russia the power to stop Nato deployments in the region.

And, on that note, we will be closing the blog for today.

Back in Dublin, the leaders are still wrapping up their talks, with at least some of them seemingly inspired by what they heard about AI today.

After their session with Mistral AI founder, Artur Mensch, France’s Emmanuel Macron has warned fellow leaders that “our independence cannot be outsourced” as he called for more investments into AI.

“We have the talent, the energy and the technology. Now we need to invest more, make decisions faster and, above all, act together,” he said.

Macron added he would host another summit with ‘tech creators’ on 2 December in Paris, co-hosted with the Netherlands’ Jetten.

Poland’s Donald Tusk struck a similarly urgent tone, saying “our next race is the AI race.”

“By joining forces, Europe gains much more scale, power and influence. By coordinating our efforts, we can all be 27 times more productive, access 27 times more services and speed up our growth,” he said.

“Let’s stop funding our ambitions separately, work together and secure Europe’s place in the AI revolution.”

Over in the Netherlands, prime minister Rob Jetten is also holding crisis talks with his coalition partners after his minority government got into trouble this week over a tax reform deal with opposition parties.

The terms of the deal angered the right-wing People’s Party for Freedom and Democracy, or VVD, which voted against the reform and expressed its frustration with the move.

Jetten skipped the EU summit in Dublin (11:52) and has been locked for several hours now with the other party leaders in hope of finding a way forward.

“No one is waiting for endless political squabbling in The Hague or for new elections, and we must do everything in our power to find a way forward together,” he told reporters.

Belgian prime minister Bart De Wever will meet his coalition partners for crunch budget talks on Friday, as they seek to agree cost-cutting measures before a Monday deadline.

De Wever, a Flemish conservative, offered his resignation to King Philippe earlier this month after his five-party coalition failed to reach a deal on the 2027 budget.

But the monarch refused to accept it and told him instead to report back on 16 November.

Coalition members are to gather on Friday evening and De Wever’s office has indicated talks could run into the weekend, AFP reported.

Spain’s two deadly train accidents this year have driven passengers away from the world’s second-largest high-speed rail network, a concern for a country heavily ⁠reliant on tourism and business ⁠travel, experts and business groups ​said, as reported by Reuters.

According to competition watchdog CNMC’s data, the number of long-distance high-speed rail passengers fell 15% in January-June to 18.7 million following January’s high-speed train collision near the village of Adamuz in southern Spain that killed 46 people, ⁠with most travellers switching to rental cars and domestic flights. Based on Reuters’ calculations, that was almost six times the impact a 30-day ​closure of the line might normally be expected to have.

The 28 October collision, which killed 69 people, shut the Madrid-Andalusia high-speed line for the second time in nine months, forcing its nearly 19,000 daily passengers to seek alternatives. The line remains ⁠closed.

The impact ‌of the latest crash is ‌yet to be quantified but is likely to be heavier, experts say, as they fear further erosion of confidence in Spain, the world’s ‌second-most visited country after France, as a safe travel destination.

“All accidents are serious, but when you have two within just nine months ... you have a strong impact on reputation,” said Juan Pedro Aznar, a researcher at Esade business school, adding that restoring confidence would require making technical fixes a political priority.

Canadian tourist Isobel Hamilton, 50, said the latest crash prompted her and her husband to change plans and travel ‌outside Madrid by bus.

“We were uncomfortable about keeping a rail trip in our plans ... Even if everything had been restored and was running normally I probably would still have chosen to take the bus.”

But despite the intense weather warnings, the storm passed over Portugal without leaving the destruction the country had widely feared in its wake, after the authorities ordered schools closed as a precaution, AFP noted.

One person was wounded and four people were evacuated as a result of the storm, national civil protection chief Mario Silvestre told a press conference, while some flooding and falling trees were recorded.

Germany has had 12 presidents since the end of the second world war, all of them men. Now, for the first time, the federal republic is on the brink of getting a female head of state. But Ilse Aigner, the lead candidate for the post, is unusual in more ways than one.

“No law degree. No marriage. No penis,” is how the left-leaning Taz newspaper put it this week – a wry, if blunt, assessment of what a break with history the 61-year-old Bavarian represents.

Friedrich Merz, Germany’s Christian Democrat (CDU) chancellor, put Aigner forward as his candidate this week after months of speculation, saying it was “high time” a woman took up the role.

If elected in January, Aigner will succeed Frank-Walter Steinmeier in what is a largely ceremonial role but nonetheless Germany’s highest state position. And as she has the backing not only of Merz’s CDU but his Social Democrat partners and the opposition Greens, little is standing in her way.

Let’s take a quick look at other news from across Europe.

The latest round of talks between Ukraine, Russia and the US were held this week in Abu Dhabi.

And Russia is [now] raising points that we have no accept? Sorry, that’s the aggressor, losing on the frontline. We should be raising our expectations, what has to be done in terms of Ukraine’s security.”

#90. Australian cricket's sandpapergate is back - and it cuts deep in a 'nation of good sports'

Pat Cummins spent over an hour fielding questions over sandpapergate on Friday

As Australian cricket captain Pat Cummins spent more than an hour being grilled by reporters over one of sport's biggest cheating scandals, the tension in the hall was occasionally pierced by muffled cheers.

Outside, a crucial match in the top tier of domestic cricket was getting under way. Cummins was clear he'd rather we all be talking about that.

But inside the nets at Cricket Central in Sydney, he faced more than a dozen TV cameras and a gaggle of journalists several times as large. His goal: to put rumours of his involvement in "sandpapergate" to rest.

"I don't want to talk about this again after today," he said. "Even if it takes all day... I'll stay here until we have no more questions."

A book by his friend and former teammate Usman Khawaja has renewed scrutiny on a 2018 plot to tamper with the ball using sandpaper in Australia's third Test match against South Africa in Cape Town.

Three players admitted they were involved at the time and were handed lengthy bans, but in a memoir released last week, Khawaja said the entire Australian team knew about the sandpaper scheme.

His claims have plunged the Australian cricket world into chaos, thrown speculation over key figures from that Test - including star bowler Cummins who has since risen to hold what many joke is Australia's most important job - and reopened wounds for fans.

Josh, 28, told the BBC he thought everyone had moved on: "It's just a bit crazy that it's all blown up again... I think it is a bit ridiculous, after eight years."

"I'm a bit bored of it," another spectator at the New South Wales vs South Australia Sheffield Shield match said.

Josh says he was surprised the scandal is back in the news

Among fans, there's frustration that Khawaja has chosen to come forward now, almost a decade on and at the start of an exciting summer, and year, of cricket. Though there's an argument there'd never be a good time for Khawaja, who retired from international cricket in January, to make these allegations.

There's worry about the impact on the men's team's performance this summer, after a lacklustre showing in South Africa last month - its first Test matches there since the 2018 incident.

And there's a sense of injustice that Australia can't seem to escape the scandal, given - according to many players themselves - how common boundary-bending on ball tampering apparently was in that era.

"It's a bit of a stitch up," 21-year-old James said.

"Every country has done it... if it wasn't us, it was probably going to be someone else."

Khawaja has said he owed it to the three players punished to tell his side of the story

Khawaja's memoir is also causing rancour among current and former players and coaches.

In it, he said he had wanted the team to take collective responsibility for the tampering after batsman Cameron Bancroft was caught with the sandpaper on field.

"I said: 'Take this as a team. We did this as a team. We knew what was going on, and we have to take it as a team'."

However Khawaja has since said he didn't want to start a "witch hunt" and has refused to answer questions on exactly who he claims knew of the scheme, and what they knew.

David Warner, who received the most severe punishment of the Cape Town trio, said Khawaja had written the truth and that Cummins' press conference denying the claims "threw him under the bus".

Steve Smith, the captain at the time who was also suspended, has been travelling overseas and is yet to say anything publicly about the claims in the book. Bancroft is also staying silent for now despite previously suggesting the bowlers knew.

But half of Australia's living cricket royalty and a slew of former coaches - some of whom have been accused of wrongdoing too - have thrown in their two cents.

And Cricket Australia is under pressure from some to reopen its investigation into sandpapergate - something chief executive Todd Greenberg has already ruled out.

Prof David Rowe of the University of Western Sydney told the BBC the saga was still so painful for many players and fans as the country sees itself as "a nation of good sports".

"The idea of being a good sport is two things. One is being good at sport, so you actually win. But the other side is that you do it honourably and fairly," he said.

"Many Australians would see themselves as above all that. That is: cheating is what other teams get up to... not the nation of good sports."

He said the revelation was a "nasty shock" which brought the country "collective shame" which lingers today.

He says he doubts that the cricket scene would be able to move on without a fresh response from the governing body, Cricket Australia.

"One of the best ways to fuel conspiracy theories and keep sandpapergate going is to essentially say 'there's no more to see here' - especially when that comes from the authorities," Rowe said.

Australia kicked off it's summer with an ODI against England on Friday

Asked about a fresh investigation, Cummins said it was a matter for Cricket Australia.

Over the marathon press conference - which one reporter suggested was the longest in Australian sporting history - Cummins was cautious, pausing often to consider his responses and to make sure they answered the questions being asked of him.

But he was also frank - using strong words to reject what he said were "vague" claims by Khawaja and admitting he was frustrated a friend had put him in this position. "Friends aren't casual with each other's lives and reputations... That's something I've been grappling with the last couple of days."

As the reporters ran out of steam, Cummins said it was "annoying" that sandpapergate scandal was still a distraction for Australian cricket. He gestured outside to the Sheffield Shield match and mentioned Friday's one-day international against England in Perth, from which he and other key players are being rested.

"It's been eight years... Hopefully today we put a line through it and we're done and we can all move on to much more fun topics, like actual cricket."

"It's going to be hard to move on, given that there's two sides of the story and it doesn't look like either side is going to back down," James said.

#89. Israel’s Arrest of Journalist Abed Abu Shehadeh Raises Concerns

Abed Abu Shehadeh, a Palestinian citizen of Israel, was detained without being charged with a crime, raising concerns that the domestic security service is overstepping its authority.

Abed Abu Shehadeh, a 38-year-old journalist and a Palestinian citizen of Israel, said he was putting on his pajamas on the evening of Nov. 3 when police officers and intelligence agents burst into his family’s third-floor apartment in Jaffa.

They strip-searched him, confiscated his phone, allowed him to dress and then whisked him away, he said in an interview at a restaurant in the city.

Five days later, after interrogations by Israel’s domestic security agency, the Shin Bet, Mr. Abu Shehadeh, who lives with his wife and two young children, was let go.

A former elected member of the Tel Aviv-Jaffa City Council, Mr. Abu Shehadeh has not been charged with a crime. The Israeli authorities have not publicly offered a reason for his arrest, though court records reviewed by The New York Times show that he was accused of being in contact with an unnamed “foreign agent.”

His highly publicized arrest — a week after Israel’s national elections — came as Prime Minister Benjamin Netanyahu and his allies have claimed that foreign actors illegally funneled large amounts of money to bribe Arab citizens to vote for Arab political parties.

Critics say that Mr. Netanyahu and his allies are seeking to undermine the legitimacy of the record gains by Arab parties in the Parliament, or the Knesset, by contending without basis that Arab voters stole the election.

#88. Former Hamas bodyguard convicted of Birmingham knife crime also wanted in Germany

Mohammed Alfatih rearrested over alleged plot to target Israelis in Europe while detained for attack on bailiff

A former Hamas bodyguard convicted of knife crime in the UK is wanted in Germany for allegedly plotting to murder Jewish people.

Details of the alleged plot can now be reported after Mohammed Alfatih, also known as Mohammed Naim, was found guilty on Friday of a knife attack on a bailiff outside his home in Birmingham.

The 40-year-old was found guilty by a majority verdict of unlawful wounding, possession of two knives and criminal damage. He was cleared of the more serious charge of wounding with intent.

He was under surveillance by a counter-terrorism unit when he attacked the bailiff last year.

Alfatih, the son of a senior Hamas official, could not be named during his trial because of reporting restrictions.

He had been photographed working for a former leader of Hamas before being granted asylum in the UK in 2015 and British citizenship in 2020.

While detained for the attack on the bailiff in Birmingham, he was rearrested after authorities in Germany said he had been part of an alleged plot to attack Israeli targets in central Europe.

Alfatih, who was born in Germany, is expected to be extradited to the country after he has served his sentence in the UK, with his connection to Hamas stated in the German extradition request.

German prosecutors charged seven suspected Hamas members in September over an alleged plot to target Jews or Israelis in Germany or Austria around the second anniversary of the 7 October attack on Israel.

They said “Mohammed A” played a prominent role in transporting and storing weapons but was being prosecuted separately after UK authorities arrested him in London and noted he had not yet been returned to Germany.

German authorities said he was connected to Hamas’s leadership and was said to have produced a video in advance to take responsibility for the attack on its behalf.

The attack was disrupted by Germany’s domestic intelligence service after a tipoff from the Mossad, Israel’s secret service.

During a two-week trial at Birmingham crown court, the jury was shown video footage of Alfatih lashing out with a knife at the broken window of a van on 14 October 2025.

Sign up for the Breaking News UK email to get newsletter alerts direct to your inbox

The bailiff, Kevin Bucknall, was left with cuts to his head and required hospital treatment. The defendant pleaded not guilty on the grounds of self-defence.

The jurors, who convicted Alfatih after being shown footage of him brandishing two large kitchen knives outside his home in Kings Heath, Birmingham, were not told of his links to Hamas.

They were also not told Alfatih was under surveillance by a counter-terrorism unit after being released on bail on suspicion of a terror offence days before the bailiff was attacked.

A government source said: “This is a staggering example of the loss of border control under the last Conservative government, who granted his asylum claim and gave him citizenship.

“This Labour government has just introduced reforms that ensure those who gain citizenship must first show they are a good citizen and have contributed to this country.”

#86. FAA issues US national ground stop on all flights

The US federal aviation regulator announced a nationwide ground stop for all airports. Federal officials blamed a communications outage and said technicians are investigating.

The US Federal Aviation Administration (FAA) on Friday issued a ground stop on all national flights. The FAA is the US government agency responsible for air traffic control.

"All departing traffic should remain groundstopped until further notice," the FAA said.

The agency said the ground stop is "due to extraordinary circumstance and reasons for safety." The FAA cited a temporary telecommunication outage and said, "Every flight has been accounted for and international flights will be handled on a case by case basis."

The agency later said flights were halted "out of abundance of caution."

In a post on X, US Secretary of Transportation Sean Duffy said the stop was due to a communication issue impacting all Air Route Traffic Control Centers.

"FAA technicians are investigating the issue. We will provide immediate updates as we have them," he wrote.

US Homeland Security Secretary Markwayne Mullin told reporters at the White House that there was no indication this was an attack against the US.

"This is a telecommunications outage," Mullin said. "All the flights are accounted for."

"Everybody's safe. There's no reason to be concerned about your loved ones if they're flying," he added.

According to the FlightAware aviation tracker, nearly 7,000 flights in the US were delayed or canceled due to the ground stop.

Canada's air navigation service provider, NAV Canada, said it is closely monitoring the US ground stop and later said, "It was implementing traffic management measures at Toronto Pearson, Montreal-Trudeau, Calgary and Vancouver to safely manage traffic volumes."

"Travelers should expect delays and check with their airline. Our systems are operating normally," NAV Canada added.

Toronto Pearson Airport said flights between Toronto and the US have been impacted by the ground stop.

It's the first nationwide ground stop since January 2023, when the FAA dealt with a system outage. Prior to that, the last ground stop was after the September 11, 2001 terror attacks.
